Thank you for your letters of 7th and 8th August.

[ am sorry that the government is taking no interest in the crop circle
phenomenon. I. feel that perhaps it should do so. Japanese universities have
thought it worth funding a massive research programme in this country, including
the provision of much equipment and the maintenance of a team of 19 scientists
in viltshire for several weeks this summer. The British could be missing a
trick...

Be that as it may, the phenomenon appears to be taking an interest in the
government ! I enclose (for you to keep) an aerial photograph of a recent event
which occurred on the Chequers estate.

You may also like to have the enclosed newspaper cutting.

If at some. future date government agencies decide to study the phenomenon,
CCCS will remain ready to contribute to research.

Yours sincerely,

Corn circles: back to square one

ORN CIRCLES pave been

cropping up with ever in-
creasing frequency and com-
plexity in recent months (Envi-
ronment Guardian, August 2).
Yet few writers or scientists
address such a potentially seri-
ous subject seriously, for fear of
ridicule.

We are left with the self- or
government-appointed de-
bunker who attempts to explain
it all away with some pseudo-
scientific theory. In this case
the man is Doctor Terence Mea-
den and the theory is called
“plasma ring vortices”.

This sounded all very plausi-
ble so long as corn circles
remained circles, but they
haven't. Dr Meaden stretch

ed
straight lines in the corn, but |

he never bargained for the im-
mensely complex pattern that

Jappeared at Barbury Castle, | Kent.

Wiltshire, on July 17, 1991.
From that day on he must have
regretted that he had not at-
tempted to debunk the occur-
rences by claiming they were a
hoax perpetrated by students.
Anyone but a fanatic ‘must
know that s guch a pattern nis the
product lligent beings. |
Whether those beings are of the
earthly student variety or of
extra terrestrial origins is an-
other question altogether. Let
us take the subject. seriously
and above all let us embarrass
those few ‘shadowy people: in |:
government who do know the |:
origins of-corn circles into shar- |:
ing their information with us
instead of protecting us from it.
We'might be able to cope with it |.
more- responsibly than they.
‘That would not be too difficult.
(Sir) John Whitmore.

Thank you for your letter of 6 August, on the subject of a recent crop circle
occurrence in Cambridgeshire. This letter crossed in the post with my reply to
your earlier letter.

As I explained in this previous letter, there is no Ministry of Defence
involvement in crop circle research. JI can confirm that an RAF aircraft was in
the area at the time you mentioned, but, again, can give you a catagoric
assurance that its presence was entirely unconnected with the presence of. any
crop circles. RAF aircraft routinely fly from bases in this area, and there was
nothing out of the ordinary about this flight.

Once again, I wish you luck with your studies.

Yours § meaty

CENTRE FOR CROP CIRCLE STUDIES

en |

Thank you for your letter of 26th July.

Perhaps I might add to my letter of 15th July that a recent crop circle
occurrence near Spaldwick, Cambridgeshire, which we think can be dated to the
night of 27/28 July, seems to have been of close interest to a Nimrod aircraft
(possibly of No. 51 Squadron), which made five low passes across it shortly
after sunset on Monday 29th July. We have other anecdotal evidence of what seems
to be more than casual interest in these events by military aircraft.

I am, again, writing not to complain but to express the readiness of CCCS to
contribute to any government research which is being understaken. Our own aerial
surveys of these events, coupled with our field research, indicate some strongly
energetic local effects of an unusual kind both before and after crop formations
have occurred.

We believe these events are of importance. We would welcome (with suitable
discretion) your confirmation that government research is in train. I repeat
cccs's offer to contribute to serious study of the phenomenon.

Yours sincerely,

7

(Ralph Noyes)

Centre for Crop Circle Studies Our reference

7 Kogust 1992

Veuw Mr Noyes,

My predecessor wrote to you on 26 July, with an interim reply to your letter of
15 July 1991 to the Under-Secretary of State for the Armed Forces. I am now
able to provide the substantive reply that you were promised.

I should state at the outset that the Ministry of Defence has no interest in
crop circles, and is conducting no research or investigations into this
phenomenon. You mentioned the involvement of the Army Air Corps, and Lt Col

Clearly, there have been instances of individuals taking photographs
of crop circles, but this has only occurred during routine training flights.
Individuals are entirely free to pursue their personal interests either in their
own time, or in a manner that is consistent with Service regulations. There can
be no question of MOD resources being used to support investigation of the crop
circle phenomenon. :

You also mentioned that a Chinook helicopter was in the Beckhampton-Marlborough
area on the night of July 11. I can confirm that a Chinook was in the area at
the time, but can assure you that it was conducting a standard training sortie.
Its presence in the area was unconnected with crop circles, and there is no
question of our having any theory about the formation of crop circles, or indeed
any pool of information on the phenomenon.

I hope this is helpful, although I realise it is not the response that you were

hoping for. I should like to take this opportunity to wish you and the Centre
for Crop Circle Studies luck with your investigations.

Vane) Si ely ;

Y INVO ENT IN CO cr ES STIGATION
1. Thank you for your minute Sec(AS)12/3 of 1 August.

2. Frankly I am reluctant to start firing off edicts about what
AAC pilots can and can't do when they spot corn circles beneath
their helicopters - I am pretty sure that I wouldn't be taken
seriously if I did.

3. I think it is already generally accepted that MOD resources
must not be used specifically in pursuit of personal interests.
I do not, however, see that the act of taking photographs of corn
circles during routine training flights - which incidentally
frequently entail maintaining a low hover in a chosen position -
consumes any additional resources whatsoever. Neither do I see
any difficulty in such photographs being made available to
organisations concerned with solving the mystery, provided no
impression was given that they formed any part of an official MOD
investigations.

ARMY INVOLVEMENT IN CORN CIRCLE INVESTIGATIONS

Reference:
A. D/GS Sec 41/2 dated 30 duly 1991
B. D/Sec(AS)12/3 dated 1 August 1991

1. Thank you for the opportunity to contribute to the debate
on service involvement in corn circle investigations.

2. I am unaware of any instances in which the RN has helped
investigators by taking aerial photographs of these circles
and I very much doubt whether any such requests have been
received. However, I feel that I would have some difficulty
persuading the Navy that they should not co-operate where
possible on the basis of the advice contained in reference B.

3. Are we not being over-sensitive towards the UFO lobby
here? While our long standing policy of not devoting time or
resources to investigating UFO reports is without question
sensible, there is surely a need to draw a distinction
between bizarre and unconfirmable reports of incidents which
probably never occurred and a mildly interesting natural
phenomenon the existence of which is patently obvious. It
seems odd that we should impose an artificial constraint on
servicemen to avoid any association with crop circles simply
because of the antics of the lunatic fringe. After all, as
your guidelines stipulate, there is no harm in providing
assistance where this does not involve the expenditure of
extra time or effort. I am quite happy to see RN helicopters
overflying these objects should they be asked to do so and
intend to register no objection should any requests come my

way.

‘Sec(NS)b

ARMY INVOLVEMENT IN CORN CIRCLE INVESTIGATIONS

Reference: D/GS Sec 41/2 dated 30 July 1991

1. Thank you for your minute at Reference in which you set out the position
with regard to Army involvement into the investigation of corn circles.

2. The fact that photographs have been taken from Army Air Corps
helicopters puts us in a difficult position, even if this was done during
routine training flights. The MOD’s position with regard to UFOs is that we do
not devote time or resources to investigation of such reports, unless an
incident has any defence significance. For the AAC to involve themselves in
this way erodes this position, as the UFO lobby will see this as active
investigation of UFO-related activity (as they view corn circles) - something
that we have categorically and publicly stated that we do not do.

3. I would be grateful if you would have a look at this, and give some
thought to whether it would be possible to steer the AAC away from getting
involved with the investigation of corn circles.

4. Of course, none of this would in any way prevent AAC personnel getting
involved in their own time, provided that Service/MOD resources were not used.

5. I would be grateful for your rapid advice, as I believe we should ensure
that our policy is consistent before we reply to the letter from the Centre for
Crop Circle Studies.

6. I am copying this minute to Sec(NS), who may wish to ensure that Naval
helicopters are not similarly involved.

ARMY INVOLVEMENT IN CORN CIRCLE INVESTIGATIONS

References: A. D/GS Sec 41/2 dated 18 July
B. D/Sec(AS) 12/3 dated 17 July

1. Further to Reference A, I attach a short piece provided by

the 40. Air Corps on the involvement of LT Col (Ret ) SERIE 0

AFC in the corn circles business.

2. You will note that Lt co] SEO interest was entirely
personal and was originally sparked by a routine low flying
complaint from a local farmer. Though a report of was made to MOD
I suspect that this was solely on Lt Col Se own
initiative. ;

3. TI have not attempted to verify the claim that a pilot from
658 Sqn took pictures of corn circles ~ I am sure we can take Mr

_Noyes’s word for it, I imagine that this is just another example
of an AAC pilot taking a personal interest in the phenomenon.

4, I agree with your suggested line of reply (Reference B,
paragraph 4), but you might consider developing it along the
following lines. .

MOD has no interest in the corn circles phenomenon and
therefore has nothing to offer in the way of information
exchange. However, as they have done in the past, individuals
in the Services are free to pursue their personal interests
either in their own time or in a manner that is consistent
with normal training objectives. If, for example, Army Air
Corps pilots take photographs of corn circles during routine
training flights they would be entirely free to share the
results with organisations concerned with solving the
mystery. Nor would MOD object to such organisations
cultivating their existing individual contacts with the
Services, provide it was understood that no MOD resources
could be made available for the sole purpose of corn circle
research.

5. I hope this helps, and I would be grateful for a copy of your
reply.

INVOLVMENT OF LT COL (RETD) AFC

In 1985 Lt Col SRQSMSMEMN then SOl STANDARDS/NIGHT SAFETY HQ DAAC, was
contacted by a local farmer asking what the AAC had been doing to his corn.
Taking the Technical Accident Investigation Officer (AIFSO) with him he
visited the area which was about 2 miles from Middle Wallop. It turned out
to be a perfect set of circles in the corn.

Neither he nor the AIFSO could find any sensible explanation for the rings
accept to state categorically that it was nothing to do with helicopters.
ColBienele submitted a factual report to MOD, accompanied by good
photographs, stating what they saw and their inability to explain it.

A local man, Mr Colin Andrews (author of Circles in the Corn) a member of

a group who had been investigating the circles for some years, also turned
up on the scene. He invited Col SESRSRNTN « o attend a meeting at which an
theories could be put forward for the meeting to shoot down. ORs eci0n 40 |
was asked to attend as a helicopter agent to debate the inevitable “It's

HELICOPTERS" theories. This he did and found the meeting to be very sensible
and level headed. He told the chairman the ghist of his report. /

fempsection 40 | became intrigued and that year, 1985, passed the word round
to UK based AAC units to let him know if they saw any circles and give him
the Grid Ref. There were one or two responses and then interest waiwed.

feepy Section 40 | was subsequently approached by BBC TV Pebble Mill, who were
making a film about the circles, to again be the helicopter agent and debate
the helicopter theories. This he did, and laid on a demonstration with a
helicopter in long grass to conclusi i trate the facts. This was
all filmed and shown on TV. Lt Col MERMOMRA continues to take an inactive
interest in the circles and would be delighted to be given a satisfactory

explanation for them.

ATC OCCURRENCE REPORT — PROJECTILE - 17 JUN 91

Reference: MATO/20/6/6/RS3A dated 2 Jul 91

1. Thank you for your letter at Reference in which you asked for our
assistance with the investigation into an incident where a "wingless projectile"
was sighted by four airline passengers on 17 June.

2. Having spoken to the Meteorological Office, I have discovered that a
weather balloon was launched from their Crawley Centre at 1715 GMT on the date
in question. At 1719 GMT it would have attained a height of around
4000-5000feet, and by 1735 GMT it would have reached about 22000 feet.

3. Obviously there are some discrepancies; the timing is slightly out, and
the balloon would have been rising. Also, the description does not tie in, as
the balloon would have had equipment attached to the underside. I am not sure
how the precise locations tie in. However, the times and locations are
sufficiently close to suggest that this explanation is a possibility.

4. I hope this is helpful.

Yours Sawy

Squadron Leader Section 40, | RAF
Dear Squadron Leader Section 40]

I wish to thank you very much for your very courteous reply to
my letter of inquiy, it is very rare indeed to be able to com-
municate with the over all senior British RAF liaison officer
at 3AF RAF Mildenhall.

My understanding of the current situation in regard to UFU/un-
known light phenomena is, that of simple established protocol .
procedure between your government and the US government in ma-
tters of interest to your RAF and MOD. Is this interpetation a
correct one of your established liaison with the USAF in rega-
rd to on base sightings of UFO/unknown light phenomena, by ba-~
se personnal. ; ; ,

Mindfull of this fact and the problem of maintaining good com-
munication and good relations with the USAF people over in En-

gland, there must no doubt surface some very sensetive situat-
jons where memo's on sightings must be treated in strict conf-
idence, as they pertain to your Public Records Act. Do you ha-
ve any high level memo's from the USAF people at the command
Level which deal with the need to remain out of the public
access area. I must weigh the fine balance of the need to know
situations of these British UFO enthusiast with the need for
strict interpetation of your Public Records Act.

This regulation is very good in the fact that it guarantees
complete assurance that once a memo on a sighting is forwarded
by yourself then it must assure that the memo remains out of
bounds for the prying eyes of the tabuloid newspapers and also
unfortunately the very interesting memo is also locked up for
30 years, which eliminates any immediate look into the partic-
ular typ of phenomena. I am.doubtfull a. freedome of informat~
jon act regulation would apply.to memo's addressed to your
department from thé people at the USAF base at RAF Mildenhall.

Is my interpetation of your regulations correct in the fact
nothing in the form of internal memo's of communication betwe-
en the USAF people at RAF Mildenhall and your office, regarded
as under the Official Secrets Act as well as the Public Recor-
ds Act which makes them allthough from a USAF source, exempt
from any foia request by a US civillian UFO enthusiast. One
simple explanation is that the USAF originated memo is retyped
by your secretary and allthough the information is originally
from the USAF people, it is now on stationary from your secti-~
on dept with your letter of forwarding to the MOD, this makes
the document a MOD internal memo. Would this also be correct.
Here, on the subject of local community relations with local
newspapers, the East Anglian Daily Times at 30 lower Brook St,
Ipswich, Suffolk, England IP4 1AN. It would be expected that a

I am pleased to be able to respond to your request for information dated
7 May 1983. As you may now know, the 513th Combat Support Group provides
document management services for Headquarters, Third Air Force. After. extensive
effort, we are able to successfully answer your four separately directed requests
for information concerning unexplained lights on 27 December 1980.

It might interest you to know that the US Air Force had no longer retained
a copy of the 13 January 1981 letter written by Lt Col Charles I. Halt. The
Air Force file copy had been properly disposed of in accordance with Air Force
Regulations. Fortunately, through diligent inquiry and the-gractous consent of
Her Majesty's government, the British Ministry of Defence and the Royal Air
Force, the US Air Force was previded a copy for you. We trust this adequately
explains the initial inability to provide a favorable response.

As you also asked, we have attached such documentation as we had concerning
the processing of your several FOIA requests as of receipt of your 7 May 1983
request. :

Thank you for your letter of 15 July 1991 on the subject of
Unidentified Flying Objects (UFOs).

I read the ‘Branton’ document which you enclosed with your
letter, and having done so, I can confirm that there is nothing
in it that would be of interest to the MoD. As you know, our
sole concern is to determine whether or not UFOS present a threat
to the security and defence of the United Kingdom and unless we
judge that they do, and this is not normally the case, no attempt
is made by MoD to investigate or identify the objects. Also, I
can confirm that the MoD. does not posess any positive evidence to
suggest that ‘alien spacecraft’ have landed on the planet.

I would agree that the material presented in the ‘Branton’
document reads like science fiction. No attempt is made anywhere
within the document to provide factual evidence for any of the
assertions made about the UFO phenomena. I would like to assure
you that there is no question of attempting to cover up any
incident, nor are we trying in any way to obscure the truth about

UFOs from the general public. i
I am returning the ’Branton’ document to you with this letter.

I hope this is helpful.

QR, CHAP.36, SECURITY, OFFICIAL INFORMATION AND PUBLIC RELATIONS, PARA J2448

32448. Disclosure of official information to Courts.
Instructions regarding the disclosure of information
(either. oral or documentary) to Courts or in
arbitration proceedings, and the giving of evidence
by expert witnesses, are contained in para J965 and
AP3207 (Manual of Flight Safety).

32449. Enquiries from Members of Parliament.

(i) All official enquiries made by Members of
Parliament are to be referred to the MOD for
reply. When exceptionally an immediate answer
is necessary, eg where a Member of Parliament
requests factual information in or to allay
relatives’ anxiety, reference should be made in
office hours to the Private Secretary to
US of S(AF) or US of S(DP) as appropriate, if
possible before a reply is given, otherwise as soon
as possible afterwards. Urgent enquiries out of
office hours are to be reported to the Resident
Clerk of the MOD HQ. who will notify the
appropriate Private Secretary. .

(2) Written communications on official matters
from Members of Parliament or from persons
who state that they are prospective Parliamentary
candidates must be acknowledged and the writer
informed that the matter is being referred to the
MOD for consideration and reply. The letter, or
a full account of a conversation if applicable,
together with a detailed report, is to be forwarded
without delay to the appropriate Minister’s office
in accordance with current instructions.

(3) In this regulation, the term ‘‘Members of
Parliament’? means members of both Houses of
the UK Parliament,.and of any other legislature,
including foreign. and commonwealth
legislatures. ‘

. 2450-2451. (Omitted)

SECTION 2—ACTIVITIES INVOLVING THE
USE OF OFFICIAL INFORMATION OR
EXPERIENCE
2452. General Considerations.
63) Greater openness in the work of Government
requires:

(a) The fullest possible exposition in
Parliament and to the public of the reasons for
Government policies and decisions when those
policies and decisions have been formulated
and are announced.

(Major Revision 3/ Jun, 1986)

(0) The creation of a better public
understanding about the way in which the
processes of Government work and about the
factual or technical background to
Government policies and decisions.

(2) The development of openness in this sense

does not imply a licence to Service personnel to;°

discuss with unauthorized persons Government
policies and decisions which are in the process of
being formulated before announcements are
made. Nor does it cover participation by Service
personnel in the public discussions of politically
controversial topics, whether the issues are
important or relatively trivial. {A politically
controversial issue is one which is, has been or is
clearly about to be a matter of controversy
between political parties in this country). The
exposition of Government policies and decisions
is the responsibility of Ministers. It would
therefore be improper for individual members of
the Services to be personally identified in public
with any line in conflict with declared
Government policy. There will, from time to
time, be opportunities for personnel to help in
creating a better understanding of the way in
which the processes of Government work and, in
some instances, about the factual or technical
background to Government policies or decisions.
There will also be occasions when personnel can
and should make a contribution to knowledge on
the basis of specialized information and experi-
ence obtained in the course of their official duties;
in particular, professional, scientific and engineer-
ing personnel are encouraged: to participate
in discussions with learned bodies so long as
policy, defence and commercial interests are
protected. Any such participation in public
discussion or contribution to knowledge of
this ‘kind must, however, be such as will
neither prejudice national security; create the
possibility of embarrassment to the Government
in the conduct of its policies; not bring into
question the impartiality of HM Forces. It is
essential therefore that control over what is made
public should rest with the appropriate auth-
orities of the MOD.

32453. Applicability.
(1) The regulations in paras 12452 to 32459 and
in J Appendix 43 govern the disclosure of infor-
mation to the general public or to any persons
not authorised for official purposes to have
access to official information. This includes also
the placing of further information in the hands

SECTION 2, OFFICIAL INFORMATION, ETC, PARA J2455

of any person or group without restraint on
further dissemination or discussion.

(2) The activities governed by these regulations
are, in the main:
(a) Public lectures and speeches, formal or
informal, communications to (or interviews
with) the press or other media, film, radio and
television appearances, and statements to non
Governmental bodies.
(b) Books, monographs, memoirs, articles,
letters or other textual . material, whether
purporting to be fact or fiction.

(c) Thesis for degrees or diplomas.

{d) Participation in outside study conferences,
seminars and discussions.

(3) Serving members of HM Forces who wish to
take part, anonymously or otherwise, in any
outside activity which involves the, disclosure of
information obtained in the course of official
duties, the use of official experience, or the
public expression of views on official matters,
must obtain MOD authority in advance. The
procedures for obtaining permission are set out
in J Appendix 43 (see also para 32455, Press
announcements). The responsibility for seeking
this authority in sufficient time to allow proper
consideration rests with the individual. Failure to
obtain the necessary permission before
undertaking any such activity is a disciplinary
offence.

(4) International Organisations. Members of
HM Forces who are serving in international
organisations are to observe the rules and
procedures of the organisation concerned where
the material relates to the business of that
organisation. However, since such persons also
remain subject to the provisions of Queen’s
Regulations, they are also to consult the MOD
(CPR through the appropriate DPR) particularly
whenever material of direct concern to the UK is
involved and in any case of doubt.

(5) Personnel who have left the Service.
Personnel who, after leaving HM Forces, wish to
publish any information which they have
acquired or to which they have had access as a
result of their official position or service, and
which is covered by the Official Secrets Acts,
should seek the official sanction of the MOD
before publication, as required by the
Declaration which is signed on termination of
service (MOD Form 135, reproduced in
Appendix 13).

32454. Principles.

(1) The general principles which apply to outside
activities involving the use of official
information or experience are:

(a) There must be no disclosure of classified or
“In Confidence” information.

(5) There should be no discussion of politically
controversial issues (see para J2452).

(c) The relations between Service personnel
and Ministers, or the confidential advice given
to Ministers, should not be disclosed.

(dq) There should be no comment on
individuals or organisations in terms which the
MOD wouid regard as objectionable.

(e) The activity should not conflict with the
interests of the Services, or of the MOD, or
bring their good name, or that of the Civil
Service generally, into disrepute.

(f) The commercial and patent interests of the
MOD, or other Government Departments,
and of organisations and contractors
collaborating with them, should be fully
protected. It is the responsibility of those
proposing to take part in an outside activity to
seek advice if these commercial interests might
be jeopardised in any way, and in particular if
the activity relates to work under departmental
contracts. It should be noted that disclosure,
even under conditions of confidence, to whole
sections of industry may in some
circumstances constitute publication for
patent purposes and might make it impossible
to secure valid patent protection for the
subject matter disclosed.

(g) The security or other national interests of
collaborating countries should be fully
protected in discussions about international
collaborative projects or agreements to which
HM Government is a party, even where UK
interests are not directly affected.

(A) Permission should be obtained to republish
any material covered by copyright, whether
the copyright owner is the Crown or not.
Particular care is necessary where it is
proposed to use material the origin of which is
obscure.

32455. Press Announcements.

-(1) Official communications to the Press will
normally be made by Public Relations staffs.
They may however be made by other duly
authorised personnel in British Commands,

(Major Revision 3/Jun, 1986)

QR, CHAP 36, SECURITY, OFFICIAL INFORMATION AND PUBLIC RELATIONS, PARA 32455

ships, units and establishments, eg the CO or his
representative, when the information given is
factual and relates solely to the command, ship,
unit or establishment concerned and when this is
in accordance with any separate instructions.
They must avoid commient on issues of a
politically controversial nature.

(2) Where casualties have occurred, the names of
persons killed or injured are not in any
circumstances to be divulged until 24 hours after
telegrams have been despatched informing the
next-of-kin, or until it is definitely known that
the next-of-kin have been informed, whichever is
the earlier.

42456. Broadcasts and Press Interviews.

(1) Invitations for Service personnel to take part
in a radio or television programme are usually
made by the broadcasting authorities through
official channels, but a direct approach may
sometimes be made. If an individual is
approached direct, he should immediately report
the matter as in para i of J Appendix 43.
Invitations to co-operate in the production of
programmes or for help in providing briefing
and background information for a programme
should be reported similarly.

(2) Public justification of Government policy is
a function of Ministers, not of Service personnel.
Members of HM Forces should not therefore
take part in a broadcast discussion or press
interview on the merits of a policy which is, or
may become, a matter of controversy between
the political parties. As even a factual statement
on such a subject may be open to miscon-
struction, it is preferable that personnel should
not speak in public on any politically
controversial issue (see para J2452). An
unrehearsed interview or discussion on a non-
controversial subject can easily move into an
area of political controversy and the position of
Service personnel on controversial matters must
therefore be made clear quite to the
commentator or interviewer beforehand. So as to
reduce the possibility of subsequent
misinterpretation a public relations or other
officer is to be in attendance at all Press
interviews and radio or television appearances.

32457. Outside Seminars and Study Conferences.

(i) Members of HM Forces in their official
capacity should not, in the absence of specific
permission, accept invitations to conferences

" (Major Revision 3/Jun, 1986)

convened by, or under the aegis of, party
political organisations. They may, in their
official capacity, attend or take part in non-
governmental conferences, seminars, etc, which
are not conducted under the aegis of a party
political organisation provided that the MOD is
satisfied as to the character of the conference and
the advantage likely to accrue.

(2) Personnel who receive an invitation from a
non-governmental and non-political body to
participate in a study conference, seminar or
discussion are, before accepting:
(@) If serving in MOD HQ, to seek prior
authority through the appropriate DPR.
(d) If serving outside MOD HQ, to seek the
prior authority of the C in C or Head of
Establishment, or other officer, official or
authority to whom his responsibility has been
delegated. -
In any case of doubt the Secretary of the Defence
Studies Steering Group (Head of Sec (Pol
Studies) MOD) should be consulted. Texts
should be submitted for clearance in advance
under the procedure set out in J Appendix 43.

(3) Service personnel are free to join the Inter-
national Institute for Strategic Studies (1ISS), the
Royal Institute of International Affairs (RITA),
or the Royal United Services Institute for
Defence Studies (RUSI) and may actively
participate in the proceedings of those bodies,
subject to the general considerations set out in
clauses (5) and (6), without seeking prior
approval as in clause (2). They are however
required to seek guidance if they are in doubt
about the propriety of their participation on any
particular occasion.

(4) Individuals who are nominated by the
Ministry to take part in a study conference or
seminar may make oral or written contributions
to the discussions; but if they are doubtful about
any point they should consult the Secretary,
Defence Studies Steering Group.

(5) Personnel who join the LISS, the RITA or the
RUSI, or who are nominated to take part in
study conferences or seminars on defence
problems, are encouraged to make as useful a
contribution to the discussions as possible: but
they must be discreet in what they say, bearing in
mind the nature of their subject and their
audience and the risk that their remarks may be
reported even if publicity is not expected. They
may discuss, in noncommittal and professional

SECTION 3, PUBLIC RELATIONS, PARA J2459D

terms, possible alternative policies or courses of
action and the advantages or disadvantages
which might be claimed for them: but it would be
improper for them to appear to be personally
identified with any line in conflict with
Government policy. If it is impossible to avoid
commenting ‘on politically controversial
questions: they should confine themselves to
statements of Government policy on the
questions involved and should avoid expressing
personal views. They may intervene to correct
gross errors of fact where silence might be
interpreted as acquiescence.

(6) Classified or commercially significant
information must not be revealed. Personnel are
advised to consult their security organisation if
they think it possible that security problems may
arise during a seminar or study conference.

32458. Questionnaires. Personnel should not
complete questionnaires received from publishers
of directories, employment agencies, research
investigators and the like if this would involve
disclosing detailed and significant information
about their official duties. While to name an
appointment can reveal the nature of the duties
attached to it, this regulation is not intended to
prevent personnel from replying suitably to
questionnaires from responsible publications such
as “Who's Who’. In any case of doubt the
appropriate security directorate or the local
security officer is to be consulted.

32459. MPs, Foreign Countries, Committees,
Contractors, etc. Instructions are contained in the
relevant Security Manuals about the disclosure of
information to:

(a) Members of Parliament.

(8) Foreign countries and foreign nationals.

{c) Committee members, Consultants and
Defence Lecturers.

{d) Contractors.

SECTION 3—-PUBLIC RELATIONS

52459A. Public Relations—General. While observ-
ing the need for security and the confidentiality of
many Service transactions, members of the Armed
Forces have a responsibility for maintaining good
relations with the public and the Press. The
regulations in this Chapter are supplemented by
Pam (Air) 58. By ‘‘Press’? is meant not only

newspapers and periodicals but also other
publications, radio and television, films and news-
reels, ie, all information media.

32459B. Visits by Press Representatives. COs must
ensure that instructions governing the admission of
Press representatives to Service ships, units and
establishments and the facilities to be granted in
connection with such visits are carefully observed.

32459C. Incidents liable to cause Parliamentary or
Press Comment.

(1) In general the preceding regulations deal with
the control of information and the release of it to
the Press. It is equally important, however, that
CsinC and Senior Officers and, as appropriate,
officers in command of detached units should
provide the MOD (Defence Press Office) with
the earliest possible official information of any
incidents or occurrences in which the RN, or the
Army or the RAF is involved and which may be
liable to form the subject of comment in
Parliament or in the Press as defined in para
J2459A.

(2) When it is impossible for a full account of any
incident to be rendered before the facts have
been thoroughly examined, an incomplete
account should be sent at the earliest moment,
followed in due course by any detailed report
considered necessary.

(3) Where such an incident occurs in a detached
unit, the CO is to inform the MOD direct by
immediate signal, as well as the CinC and such
other authorities as may be laid down in local
orders.

32459D. Defence Press and Broadcasting
Conmimittee. (DPBC). D Notices are addressed to
national and provincial newspaper editors, to radio
and television organisations, and to some
publishers of periodicals and books on defence and
related subjects. (D Notices are issued and
amended on the authority of the DPBC). The
Secretary of the DPBC is available at all times to
advise on questions that arise as to the application
of a D Notice to some particular set of
circumstances. Any advice requested by the Press
as a whole on the publication of items of
information which appear to come within the scope
of a D Notice should be referred to the Secretary
DPBC whose address may be obtained through
DPRS.

{Major Revision 3/Jun, 1986)

bearing in mind that someone SER ‘DoD sources’ ?) seems to have

Dear Sir,

I am writing on behalf of the Centre for Crop Circle Studies (cCCS) to
enquire whether we can be of any assistance to your own studies of the
interesting phenomenon of the unexplained disturbances which have been appearing
in fields of crops in recent years, particularly in the south west of the
country.

The presence of an Army Chinook helicopter in the area of Beckhampton-
Marlborough, Wiltshire, for a susbstantial period from about 23.30 hours last
Thursday, ith July, flying at very low level and on at least one occasion using
its searchlight, indicates that you may have a hypothesis about the causation of
the crop circle events to which we could usefully contribute from our ow
studies.

You may by now have received complaints from local residents about this
prolonged intrusion at a late hour. My own purpose in writing is not, however,
to make a complaint but to register CCCS's interest.

The Centre was founded early in 1990 to conduct systematic research inte the

puzzling sequence of events whi as you will know, first came under scientific.
study by Dr. in 1980. Several. of us have felt that Dr,
Be ccion 40 Aer Ss (with the recent assistance of Japanese scientists) to

formulate the model of an unusual atmospheric vortex which might account for the
crop circle formations have failed to produce more than, at best, a partial
answer. CCCS's explicit aim is to broaden the scope of research. Following the
establishment of several research panels last autumn, we are making a degree of
progress. We would like nothing better than to pool our information with any
data which is being collected by government organisations.

‘We are currently in cordial (though discreet) relations with the local police
in Wiltshire, whose own aerial surveys of the crop circle events are adding to
our knowledge. It. might be of value both to us, as a learned society, and to
you, as a Department with public responsibilities, if we could exchange
information with you on some mutually agreeable basis.
There is a precedent for this kind of collaboration. In August 1987 a pilot
of No. 658 Squadron, Army Air Corps, Netheravon, took some excellent aerial
photographs of crop circles near Westbury, Wiltshire. Thanks to the courtesy of
the Army Air Corps, these were made available to a research organisation which
preceeded CCCS and have since been published. The same organisation and other
civilian researchers had held a useful conference at Alresford, Hampshire, in
October 1985, which was attended by Lt.Col. Section 40 Bg Middle Wallop. A report
made to the MOD by Lt.Col. Section <0 Ie very helpfully placed on the public
record and has also since been published.

These early steps towards an exchange of information do not, unfortunately,
seem to have been followed up by the organisation concerned. CCCS believes that
it might be fruitful to resume them, particularly at local level, and
particularly in Wiltshire, where many events are currently occurring.

I and other members of my Council are at the Department's disposal if a
discussion seems useful to you.

Yours faithfully,

Odi NOys

(Ralph Noyes)

Thank you for your letter of 21 May 1991 on the subject of
Unidentified Flying Objects. (UFOS).

It is not our practice to identify MoD spokespersons by name,
however, in common with other Government Departments, it is the
role of the Press Office to liaise with the Media and give
statements on matters of Departmental interest.

Regarding the reported MoD statement on the Alitalia Air Incident
report of 21 April, I do not see how a statement confirming that
people make sighting reports to the MoD can be inconsistent with
our. stated policy on the subject of UFOs.

I regret that I am unqualified to suggest any reason why your fax
to the Daily Star newspaper should have apparently become
misdirected. I would suggest that a telephone engineer, or
perhaps the Daily Star themselves would be better placed to
provide an explanation.

You mention in your letter that RAF aircraft had recently
‘chased’ an object over the North Sea. Whilst it is not our
policy to comment on operational matters affecting the RAF, we
are able to answer specific queries you may have about particular,
sightings which have occurred recently and can let you know
whether we have a report on the sighting and if so what it
contains.

Finally, thank you for your kind invitation to attend your Leeds
conference on September 14. Given our limited involvement with
the subject of UFOs it would be inappropriate for representatives
of the Air Staff Secretariat to attend in an official capacity
and therefore I must decline your offer. However, I hope that
the conference proves to be a success.

I hope that this is helpful.

youd

Thank you for your letter of 25 June 1991 on the subject of Unidentified

As you may be aware from our previous correspondence with you, the Ministry

With regard to the unusual incident witnessed at Rendlesham Forest in December

that the events described in Lt Col Halt’s report were not of any defence
significance.

Door Spedon bod ii

Thank you for your letter of 21 May 1991 regarding the report of
an air incident on Sunday 21 April 1991. 2 am sorry it has taken
me so long to respond.

I can confirm that the Alitalia MD80 captain’s report and the
LATCC controllers’ report have been examined by those
departments within MoD responsible for the air defence of the UK.
These departments have not been able to confirm the identity of
the object sighted by the Alitalia MD80 crew.

However, we have been able to rule out any possibility that what
was seen was a missile from Army firing ranges in the Lydd area.
Additionally, we have no reports of any space related activity
which could be used to provide an explanation for what was seen,
and the description of the object given by the Aliitalia captain
does nat correspond with what would be expected if the object had
been a meteorological balloon. ;

In the absence of any clear evidence which could be used to
identify the object it is our intention to treat this sighting

like that of any other Unidentified Flying Object and therefore :
we will not be undertaking any further investigation into the

sighting.

Phank you for your letter of 6 May 1991 regarding the sighting of
an object over Kent on 21 April.

I can confirm that MoD has been asked to assist with the Civil
Aviation Authority examination of the Air Incident report made by
the Alitalia MD80 aircraft's pilot.

The only surface-to-air missiles that could go as high as 22,000
feet are the Bloodhound (operated by the RAF) and the Sea Dart
(operated by the Royal Navy). The Rapier missile is launched
from the range at Benbecula in the Western Isles and the
Aberporth range in Wales. Precise details of the operational
ceiling of Rapier missiles is classified, but they could not go
as high as 22,000 feet. The sighting in question would not be
connected with the range at Lydd, as this has only anti-tank,
light mortar, grenade and rifle ranges as well as limited field
firing facilities.

It will not be possible for MoD to send you any of the
correspondence relating to the Alitalia pilot’s Air Incident
report. However, you may wish to contact the Civil Aviation

Authority separately.

Thank you for your letter of 14 of May 1991 on the subject of Unidentified
Flying Objects (UFOs).

You requested the "technical history" of attempts in putting "flying saucers"
together.

You might find it helpful if I begin by explaining that although we do receive
and co-ordinate some information about UFO sightings, our main concern is to
determine whether or not UFOs present a threat to the security and defence of
the’ United Kingdom. Unless we judge that they do, and this is not normally the
case, no attempt is made by MOD to investigate or identify the object.

Given this limited involvement with the subject, The Ministry of Defence has

no department which is appointed solely for the purpose of studying UFO reports,
nor are there any staff employed on the subject full time. The reports we
receive, which are usually limited to very brief details of what was witnessed,
are passed to staff in departments concerned with air defence of the United
Kingdom, who examine them as part of their normal duties. The Ministry of
Defence does not carry out studies, investigations or suryeys into the UFO
phenomenon. ;

Finally, the Ministry of Defence does not possess any positive evidence to
suggest that "alien spacecraft" have landed on the planet.

I hope that this is helpful.

Yours Slaucdy,

From: REIN Secretaria (air Staff)2a, Ron aaam°

10th June 1991.

‘Mr Tom King M.P.,

The Secretary of State for Defence,
Ministry of Defence,

Whitehall,

London.

Dear Mr King,

t refer to an incident reported in ‘The Sunday Times’ dated 5th
May 1991, in which it was alledged that an. unidentified ‘missile’
almost collided with an Alitalia passenger airliner en route from
Milan to London. It was also reported that the near miss occurred
almost directly above a Ministry of Defence firing range. I
understand, however, that the Ministry of Defence has stated that
the object had not originated from any British armed forces. The
corollary of this statement is that some (assumedly unauthorised)
agency is launching projectiles in British airspace, thereby
creating a very serious threat to civilian air traffic.

I have conducted some personal research into reports of such
unidentified incursions into our airspace and it appears that
this is by no means an isolated incident,

Tt should like to know the findings of investigations into this
matter carried out by the Ministry of Defence, and what actions
Her Majesty’s Government intend to take to ensure the safety of
aircraft. I urge you to authorise a public enquiry into this and
the many similar cases of such intrusions into British airspace.

A copy of this letter has been sent to my member of Parliament,
Mr Paul Murphy M.P.

incerelys

CAA REPORT OF AIR INCIDENT NEAR LYDD ON 21 APRIL 1991

Reference: D/Sec(AS)12/2 dated 9 May 1991

1. In response Masection 40, letter dated 6 May 1991 I

would suggest the following responses to his questions 2, 3 and
4, which relate to ranges and missile capabilities:

Question 2 (I assune FAROE means surface to air missiles)

The only surface to air missiles that could go as high as 22,000
feet are the Bloodhound (operated by the RAF) and the Sea Dart
(operated by the Royal Navy).

Question 3

The Rapier missile is launched from the range at Benbecula in the
Western Isles and the Aberporth range in Wales. Precise details
of the operational ceiling of Rapier missiles is classified, but
they could not go as high as 22,000 feet.

Question 4
The sighting in question would not be connected with the range at

Lydd, which only has anti-tank, light mortar, grenade and rifle
ranges, as well as limited field firing facilities.

2. I have attached the information that I received, in case
you need additional details.

CAA REPORT OF AIR INCIDENT NEAR LYDD ON 21 APRIL 3991

Reference: D/Sec(AS)8/1 dated 21 May 1991

1. You asked for assistance with your reply to a letter froma
member of the public on the above subject.

2. Rapier is a short range air defence missile with an
operational ceiling of around 10,000 feet (I am advised that this
fact is, strictly speaking, classified RESTRICTED). It could go
higher, but not as high as 22,000 feet. I don't know if Lance
could get up that high but it is considerable larger than 3
metres, and I think it is safe to assume that we have not been
firing tactical nuclear missiles over the Kent coast! ‘

3. Surface to air missiles are fired at only two Army ranges in
UK: Manorbier in South Wales (for low level air defence weapons
only) and Benbecula in the Western Isles.

4. Lydd is one of a number of small training areas on the Kent
coast that make up part of the Cinque Ports Training Area (CPTA).
It is permanently allocated to Northern Ireland tactical training
(again RESTRICTED) and has anti-tank, light mortar, grenade and
rifle ranges as well'as limited field firing facilities.

UNCLASSIFIED

RESTRICTED

Dear Sir, Es KING owen stet way 1991

I would be grateful if you could satisfy my curiosity in respect of
the statement issued to the Independent newspaper by an MoD spokesman
published on 6th May 1991, and the other matters contained herein:

a) This is in respect of the Alitalia ‘near-miss’ on 21st April 1991
in civilian airspace above Kent.

Included with his statement was the following:

"People just don't fire missiles there, but of course, we do have
quite a few UFO reports and often people who see these things describe
them as missile or cigar-shaped, or else round, and sometimes they do
appear to be travelling with no means of propulsion.”

Is this not a contradiction-in-terms to the statutory response issued
by the MoD in relation to incidents of this nature over the years? Is
is it a new 'liberal' response by AS2, DI(55)B or P&SS? Or was it an
unqualified response by a spokesman from another department?

For research & reference purposes, I would be grateful if you could
clarify the position, and the. name and particular department which
issued the statement in the first place.

b) An equally important matter has surfaced very recently which
requires an authorititive explanation. When sending a recent 'fax'
message to the offices of the Daily Star newspaper, the recepient's
copy number came back in print as G-3, and not that of the Daily Star.

Can you afford any explanation why the Daily Star ‘fax' number was
changed to that of a Whitehall department body en-route? The relevant
material copy is available for inspection should you so require.

I appreciate that because of $8.C.I. restrictions, you will probably
not be in a position to handle this particular matter yourself, but if
it could be channeled to the appropriate persons responsible for
matters of this kind, it would at the very least make someone
‘uncomfortable’ for allowing this extraordinary 'gaff' to have
occurred in the first place.
[2]

c) Can you comment on a recent UFO incident which took place over the
North Sea, during which RAF interceptors ‘chased' an object out at
sea?

Can you confirm that controllers at West Drayton observed this
incident [which was duly processed through normal channels].

Having spoken with West Drayton control staff, can you confirm that
radar plots of the intercept are a matter of record, and will the MoD
consider making these publicly available, .as has the Belgium
government in respect of similar occurrences lately?

Finally, just to digress a little if I may. Can I extend an ‘open-
invitation’ to you and your colleagues to attend our Leeds Conference
on Saturday 14th September at the Civic Theatre? If nothing else, you
may learn just what it is you have been protecting over the years. You
don't have to make yourselves known to us, but I hope you do-a better
job of it than the C.I.A. did in Frankfurt, and the South African's in
Munich....

Assuring you of our best attention at all times, I am,

Yours faithfully,

[Editor] Quest International

P.S. We hope to have 20,000 copies of Quest International in
newsstands across the United Kingdom very shortly, should be
interesting from that moment on... .

UFO REPORT

1. The attached UFO report was received from a very excitable lady in the
early morning of 2 Jun 91. Since I and others had_been looking at the sky at
the same time and within only 8 miles ag ection OE sent a letter to her and
did not forward the report through the normal channels. That letter is also
attached.

2. On 5 Jun 91, she called my Assistant, SgtSBMN—eeexpressing thanks for my
letter. but giving a detailed description of the events. Again, she appeared to
be very excited. A further call was then received after duty hours. Another
letter (attached) was sent to her and I provide an almost verbatim transcript of
the main parts of her 30-min call to Se her description of the
events she reported initially. ecuon

3. .The events occurred from 2310 hrs on 1 Jun 91 until 0130 hrs on 2 Jun 91,
all times being local:

a. At 2310 hrs, she saw 3 lights, blue, red and white which were flashing
and moving in a circle for about 5 mins. Then a round silvery, stationary
object accelerated from the area _of the Plough star constellation towards
Teesside (to Northwest) Said she was familiar with aircraft and
their speeds and judged that it moved at the speed of sound.

b. She then saw a round sivery object like a star, about the same size as
Venus, which travelled across the Plough, then silently accelerated towards
the Whitby area (to Northeast) and vanished. (Comment - this could be
satellite).

c. At 2350 hrs, she saw a round silvery object with smaller objects
around it all zig-zagging without sound.

d. She saw the same group 5 mins later, initially slow moving but then
accelerating to very high speed.

e. Between 2355 and 0006 hrs she saw 2 shooting stars.

f. At 0006 hrs she saw a silver object fly in a straight line from Malton
to Whitby.

g. Four minutes later, she saw a stationary object high above her which
then zig-zagged off in the direction of Malton (to South). :
Section 40
4 Section 40, | told so Ana she was a self-confessed star-gazer and that

she had seen similar objects several years ago. On that occasion, she had been
visited by an RAF officer and 2 Police officers - she was then living on a farm
near Pickering. She was later visited by a professor (sic) and after that
interview she had kept relaying information to him but had stopped after some
time because she was not getting any feedback. Because of the attention she
received then SERUM admits that she had been rather frightened.

1. Squadron Meee section 40 | has. passed me all of the information of your
report from early Sunday morning. . These matters are normally passed to an
office in the Ministry of Defence but in this case the phenomenon is supported
by so much other evidence that this will not be necessary. Instead I shall
explain that evidence to you.

2. About this time of year, within an hour or two of astronomical midnight at
1 am, the sun's rays reflect off very high cloud formations and particles in the
jonosphere to produce noctilucent, or noctilucous, clouds. These can cover
large parts of the Northern skyline or can appear as very localised ‘objects!
usually in any of the colours you reported. The phenomenon can occur over quite
a long time or last just a few seconds.

3. Another effect which probably appeared that night, is that of the aurora
borealis.which not only appears as the shimmering curtain, normally in green,
red or blue, but as quite small pockets. Over this weekend, solar activity has
been very high with a major storm appearing at 6 pm on Sunday. Solar activity
does not only manifest itself as a visual event, but our radar experienced a lot
of its effects over the time in question.

4, My final piece of evidence is given personally. At 1240 am, just 30 minu-
tes before. you saw the phenomenon, I was driving Northwards on the Pickering to
Whitby road. At times the whole of the Northern sky was alight with noctilucent
clouds with it dropping occasionally to very small, blueish-white clusters.

5. I hope that this has given you enough to satisfy your curiousity on this
occasion. I wish you Juck with the rest. of your star-gazing. Did you know that
the Scarborough and District Astronomical Society is holding a Lunar Exhibition
in Scarborough Public Library on 29 June 1991, between 1 and 9 pm? Apart from
slides and pictures, the exhibition will include samples of meteorites collected
locally and a sample of moon rock. brought to Earth by the ApoeTjo astronauts.

Flight Lieutenant
for Officer Commanding

Section 40|
1. I am sorry that your rial the first officer you contacted
obviously mis-heard. Sergeant has also relayed your later discusston.

Unfortunately I was unavailable at the time you called later.

2. [am sorry that my letter of 2 June 1991 was insufficient to satisfy your
query. As I said, I also saw the sky at the same time and saw nothing unusual
for this time of year. However, I shall relay your earlier report with the
additional information you so kindly provided to the Ministry of Defence.

3. The office at the Ministry of Defence which receives all reports on
possible unidentified flying objects examines only the defence implications of
each event. It does not make exhaustive enquiries about likely causes but
checks the possibility of an aircraft being seen or whether the object could
have posed a potential threat to the realm. To carry out even these limited
checks takes a lot of effort and the manpower resources preclude.a reply being
sent to all those making reports. Only on a very few occasions will a report be
followed up with a visit or further correspondence.

4, RAF Fylingdales is unable to help you further with the events. you reported.
Our radars did not see anything unusual that night apart from the aurora men-
tioned in my earlier letter. My own description of the sky at the time of your
reports is borne out by other personnel on site.

5. If you.wish to pursue the investigation further, I believe that there are
organisations which carry out continuous research into these phenomena. They
often call us to check out their own sightings but I have no record of their

whereabouts. You may be able to contact one of these groups,who operate
throughout the country on national and local levels.

4 June 1991

Thank you for your recent undated letter on the subject of Unidentified
Flying Objects (UFOs).

You may find it helpful if I begin by explaining that while the Ministry of

- Defence does receive and co-ordinate some reports of UFO sightings, our sole
concern is to establish whether or not they pose a threat to the security of
the United Kingdom. Unless we judge that they do, and this has not been the
case so far, we do not attempt to investigate further, or to identify whatever
might have been seen.

Given this limited involvement with the subject, The Ministry of Defence has

no department which is appointed solely for the purpose of studying UFO reports,
nor are there any staff employed on the subject full time. The reports we
receive, which are usually limited to very brief details of what was witnessed,
are passed to staff in departments concerned with air defence of the United
Kingdom, who examine them as part of their normal duties. The Ministry of
Defence does not carry out studies, investigations or surveys into the UFO
phenomenon.

I am sorry that I cannot be more helpful.

Yows 3 incectly ;

Thank you for your letter of 14 May 1991 on the subject of alien life forms
and Unidentified Flying Objects: (UFOs), in which you requested information
for your study. I am sorry for the delay in replying.

You may find it helpful if I begin by explaining that while the Ministry of
Defence does receive and co-ordinate some reports of UFO sightings, our sole
concern is to establish whether or not they pose a threat to the security of
the United Kingdom. Unless we judge that they do, and this has not been the
case so far, we do not attempt to investigate further, or to identify whatever
might have been seen.

Given this limited involvement with the subject, The Ministry of Defence has

no department which is appointed solely for the purpose of studying UFO reports,
nor are there any staff employed on the subject full time. The reports we
receive, which are usually limited to very brief details of what was witnessed,
are passed to staff.in departments concerned with air defence of the United
Kingdom, who examine them as part of their normal duties.

It is clear from the reports we receive that there are many strange things to
be seen in the sky. However, we believe that rational explanations could be
found for most of them. Possibilities that spring to mind include aircraft seen
from unusual angles, weather ballons, satellites, or unusual cloud formations
to name but a few. Certainly, we have yet to receive conclusive evidence that
alien spacecraft have ever landed on the planet.

If you are interested in obtaining further information on this subject, you
might like to contact some of the many civilian organisations currently engaged
in study of the UFO phenomenon. May I suggest the following societies:

British UFO Research Association

Contact International (UK)
who I am sure will be pleased to help you further.

Onur like to wish you luck with your study, and I hope this information is ©

helpful.

Yours Sialerel ,

Thank you for your letter of 14 March 1991 on the subject of
Unidentified Flying Objects (UFOs).

I have checked our files for any sighting reports which

correspond to the date, time and location mentioned in your
letter and have found two such reports. I enclose copies of
these reports, which have had the details of the originators
removed in order to ensure that their privacy is maintained.

As you will be aware, the Ministry of Defence has only a limited
involvement in this subject and we do not have the resources to

undertake any in-depth investigations. I can, however, confirm

that we have no reason to link these sightings with a satellite

re-entry. I regret that I cannot be more positive in offering a
definitive explanation for what was witnessed.

I hope that this is helpful.

Veuss

D/Sec(AS)12/3

23 April 1991

Thank you for your recent undated letter on the subject of Unidentified
Flying Objects (UFOs). I-have been asked to reply.

You may find it helpful if I begin by explaining that while the
Ministry of Defence does receive and co-ordinate some reports of
unusual sightings, our sole concern is to determine whether or not
they present a threat to the security of the United Kingdom. Unless
we judge that they do, and this has not been the case so far, we do
not attempt to investigate further, or to identify whatever might
have been seen.

Given this limited involvement with the subject, the Ministry of
Defence has no department which is appointed solely for the purpose
of studying UFO reports, nor are there any staff employed on the ;
subject full time. The reports we receive, which are usually
limited to very brief details of what was witnessed, are passed to
staff in the departments concerned with the air defence of the
United Kingdom, who examine them as a part of their normal duties.

It is clear from the reports we receive that there are many strange
things. to be seen in the sky. However, we believe that rational
explanations could be found for most of them, including aircraft

seen from unusual angles, and other phenomena you mention. I can
assure you that there is no question of attempting to "cover ‘up".

any incidents, nor are we trying in any way to obscure the "truth"
about UFOs from the general public. On the contrary, I would suggest
that our sometimes rudimentary replies are a product of the lack of
significance the subject holds within MOD caonpared to other more
pressing matters.

'

I hope that you will find this helpful.

Youn Su

The SERC Enquiries Department at Swindon passed your letter of 31 July to
me for comment. I have some difficulty from your letter in understanding
the detailed nature of, your particular interest. One possible approach
might be to contact one of the University of London Colleges which have
interests in radio systems such as the Electrical and Electronic Engineering
Department at either Imperial College, University College or Queen’ Mary
College.

_ Alternatively you might like to write in greater detail about your —
Specific interests. .

I hope this would be of assistance.

‘Head of the Radio Communications Research Unit.

; I received your communication towards the end of August and have read
it twice before replying. There is no doubt that electromagnetic fields are
incident on us all. The effect of the fields would not, however, produce the
results you describe. I suggest you must therefore look for another explana-
tion of the phenomena.

Yours sincerely,

I was very pleased tu receive your letters requesting that I be involved in your personal research.
However, I regret to inform you that, due to family commitments and to the heavy work-load resulting
from the extensive research that we are already carrying out in many areas, it will not be possible to
start any research in the area which you describe. I will be very heavily involved in European projects;
I have just. been asked to organise some activities in Europe, and will be travelling there very often.

Consequently I will not have time to do research in the areas in which you are interested.
I wish. you all the best in pursuing your. personal research.

Yours sincerely,

Your ref
Date 12 February 1991

Thank you for your letter of 2 February to my colleague
WaSection 40 \igske has recently left this organisation.

t have read your letter and enclosures with great interest and
sympathy. I am very sorry you are suffering from pressures and
are experiencing sharp physical shocks, particularly on your head
which must be extremely unpleasant.

I have to say that I think it is very unlikely indeed that you are
being made the unwitting subject of any kind of scientific
research involving radio in the way you describe. You have
already been assured by the Ministry of Defence that they have no
knowledge of any such matter. I can equally assure you that I
have no knowledge whatsoever of any research of this nature being
conducted. In the course of our radio regulatory duties, we in
this Agency have occasion to carry out a substantial amount of
monitoring of the use of the airwaves - we have an international
monitoring station which is open continuously on a 24-hour basis -
and we have no evidence of the existence of the kind of
international operation you describe.

you say that you believe a device has been implanted either in a
tooth cavity or in your right knee, which enables you to transmit
and receive conversations. I assume you have some metal tooth-
fillings and have had an operation on your knee. It seems clear
also from your notes that the "messages" you receive are in
Arabic, associated with Bahrain, and occasionally deal with
military subjects.

There have been reports over the years of very rare cases in which
a tooth-filling acts as a detector of a radio signal, which is
apparently transmitted to the brain and "heard" by a process that
is imperfectly understood. To produce such an effect the
transmitter would need to be very powerful and close to you. TI
notice you live in W2, which is of course an area containing
several foreign embassies, many of which have powerful

Pn Bataan iratiane Anoncy is an Executive Aaancy of the Department of Trade and Industry

~4

transmitters to communicate with their home countries. I am not
sure when you moved to your present address, but I see from your
papers that at one time you lived in the Rugby/Warwick area, which
as it happens also contains a number of powerful BBC transmitters.

It seems just possible that you are being affected by nearby radio
transmissions in this way (it would be interesting to know whether
you experience the same effects when you_are away from home).
However, I see you have been advised by Section 40 who. is a
distinguished figure in international radio, a e possibility
that the cause of your problem could be something other than radio
waves should not be ruled out, and that a further medical
examination might be wise. Air Vice-Marshal Ernsting has given
similar advice. Like these leading authorities, I believe this
may be your best course.

I am returning your stamped addressed envelope, with thanks, since
we have our own pre-franked stationery.

Yous peneethay

M J MICHELL

Thank you for your letter of 17 February which I have read with
interest and shown to a number of colleagues. I am grateful to
you for the further details about the unfortunate problems you are
experiencing. However, having studied the evidence in your letter
carefully, we are all here of the opinion that. the voices and

pressures you are experiencing are most unlikely to be caused by
radio wave emissions.

As you. say, I am sure it would be helpful to discuss your problems
with an expert. I would again counsel you to see your doctor
(perhaps a hospital clinic if you do not wish to. see your normal
doctor?) Alternatively I believe that The Dulwich Heatth Society
(130 Gipsy Hill SE19) has an interest in problems such as yours
and you might find it helpful to contact them.

M J MICHELL
Chief Executive

Director Pro’

Thank you for your letter of 21 January. You will probably not
be surprised that I get letters not very different from yours
from time to time.

My problem is that it is almost impossible to do the right thing
in relation to them. If I do not answer you would think of me as
yet another individual who at worst is part of a conspiracy
against you and at best is just unsympathetic. I would like to
appear under neither guise, but would you believe me if I told
you that what you believe to be happening to you is
technologically impossible.

You could do some simple experiments to demonstrate to yourself
whether radio signals could be the cause of your sensations. For
instance, the London Underground, where it runs through tunnels

deep below the surface, and where the lining of the tunnels are

made of thick steel, would be totally impervious to radio signals
originating either from a satellite or from some ground station.
Does the phenomenon stop when you use the underground? Be fair.

Another experiment would be to persuade a friend to generate a
high level of radio interference close to you which would
effectively jafa the signal which you believe you receive.

If such an experiment was done properly you would. report whether
you thought the signal was there or not without knowing whether
the jamming device was on or off. If such a "blind" trial were
to show that jamming the signal made no difference to your
sensations, would you accept that your theory was no longer
tenable?

On a much more mundane level, there have been published
instances, I believe, where powerful radio stations have
allegedly been received by individuals due to rectifying contacts
in tooth fillings. This is just about possible, because all.
sorts of. bad contacts can have “crystal set" like properties, but
even here there is a problem about’ how the electrical signals are
coupled to the nervous system so as to produce an auditory
effect.
I am sorry that this reply has taken rather longer to reach you
than I indicated on the telephone. I had indeed written most of
it then, but buried it on my desk.

Yours sincerely

MINISTRY OF DEFENCE Room Seca 40
Main Building. Whitehall London SW1A 2HB

Deer

Thankyou for your letter of 4 March on the subject of Unidentified Flying Objects
(UFOs), with which you enclosed a copy of a report about a Soviet UFO study.

To answer your first question, in 1990 the Ministry of Defence received some 258
reports of unusual sightings.

Turning to your questions in connection with the report, as you will already know,
the Ministry of Defence is only interested in reports originating in the United
Kingdom. I am not aware of any liaison with any foreign Governments, or any
exchange of information on this topic, although of course we work closely with
other nations on other areas of-mutual interest. I am unable to say whether there
is any information @n this Soviet study on our files, as all files relating to that
pericd are currently held in archives, and therefore we have no easy access to
them. However, I would have to say that I consider this unlikely, because, as I
have said, we do not co-operate with other Governments on this subject.

I hope this is helpful.

e 66/2

GE3 (RAF)
UFO REPORTS - ENQUIRIES FROM A MEMBER OF THE PUBLIC

References: A) RAF West Drayton Z6F 2107302 Feb 91
B) Sec(AS) UFO report 2111002 Feb 91

Thank you for your letter of 14 February 1991 on the subject of
Unidentified Flying Objects (UFOs).

As I mentioned in my letter of 7 February, the sighting reports
we receive are referred to our staff in the departments which are
responsible for the air defence of the United Kingdom, and they
examine the reports as part of their normal duties. There is
nothing further I can usefully add-on this particular topic.

I believe that the 'White Paper’ you refer to is the ’Statement
on the Defence Estimates’ which is published by HMSO on a yearly
basis. This publication, which comes in two parts, is obtainable
from:

HMSO Publications Centre
PO Box 276
London SW8 5DT

ve:

The 'Statement on the Defence Estimates’ is also obtainable at
HMSO bookshops, or should be available through your local public
library. mo

Additionally, you asked for ‘non-operational’ information on the
defence of UK airspace. I would suggest that you look to |
published sources such as ‘Jane’s Defence Weekly’, and (again) to

your local public library.

-

Thank you for your letter of 15 January 1991 on the subject of
Unidentified Flying Objects (UFOs).

Although we do receive and co-ordinate some information about

UFO sightings, the Ministry of Defence’s sole concern is to
determine whether or not UFOs present a threat to the security

and defence of the United Kingdom. Unless we judge that they do,
and this has not been the case so far, no attempt is made by MoD ~
to investigate or identify the objects.

The Ministry of Defence has no Department which is appointed
solely for the purpose of studying reports of UFOs and we have no
staff who are employed on the subject full time. The sighting
reports we receive are referred to our staff in the departments
which are responsible for the air defence of the United Kingdom,
and they examine the reports as part of their normal duties.

Yours Sercateles 5

From: BEQMROMN, Secretariat (Air Staff)2a, Peng section 4m

I hope this is helpful.

Thankyou for your letter of 29 December. 1990 to the Secretary of State of
Defence on the Subject of Unidentified Flying Objects (UFOS).

I am afraid that there is little I can add to my letter to you of
1 November 1990, in response to yours of 1 October. In general the reports the
Ministry of Defence receives are not filed in such a way as to make it easy to
identify those originating in a particular area. However, a search of our file
has not revealed any recent reports originating in Cornwall.

I-would be grateful if you would send any future queries direct to the

address above. I hope this is helpful.

Thankyou for your letter of 14 December on the subject. of Unidentified
Flying Objects_, (UFOS), which was received in this office on 7 January, in which
you requested ‘ar ‘any assistance we might be able to provide with regard to two
reports of unusual sightings in December.

regulations in force there. I would also consider it unlikely that RAF aircraft
could have caused these sightings as both occurred outside normal RAF operating
hours. Of course I cannot answer for civil operators

Finally, I can confirm that we did not receive any reports to corroborate

those of your witnesses.

Dear Sir,

I am the Area Regional Co-Ordinator for Quest International,
a. private investigative bureau based in the United Kingdom
which is dedicated to the scientific research of Unidentified
Flying Object phenomenon.

I recently received through my investigation unit two separate
sighting reports that occurred on December 23rd and 28th 1990.
Following automatic procedure I contacted Bristol Airport's ATC
and the regional police control covering the area's involved
with the sightings to ascertain further information which would
be useful with our investigations.

I also contacted RAF St Athan who were very helpful with my
enquiries and have been advised by their Flight Lieutenant

for Officer Commanding to contact your office with the purpose
of acquiring further information regarding these particular
incidents.

Please find enclosed all relévant details concerning the said
sightings. If you have any other UFO sighting reports involving
Bristol or the surrounding area's and feel they may be of use

to our investigations I would be very grateful if you could
forward them to me. Any information you do submit will be

held in the strictest of confidence and used only to help with
our research.

Thank you for your letter of 8 December on the subject of Unidentified
Flying Object (UFOs), in which you requested assistance in gaining
access to reports.

I am unsure of what help we can offer you with your request. As you
are aware, reports received by the Ministry of Defence are subject

to the Public Records Act, which prohibits their general release for
thirty years. Although I am not an expert, I would imagine that the
same rules apply to Home Office material, and thus, by definition, to
Police Records. Certainly, it is not within our power to grant a
police force authority to release any reports they may receive. You
might care to contact the Home Office for a clearer statement of
their policy. :

I am sorry that I cannot be more helpful.

Youd of

Thank you for your letter of 17 November on the subject of —
Unidentified Flying Objects (UFOs).

It may be helpful if I begin by explaining that while the
Ministry of Defence does receive and co-ordinate some information
about UFO sightings, our sole concern is to determine whether or
not they present a threat to the security and defence of the
United Kingdom. Unless we judge that they do, and this has not
been the case so far, no attempt is made by MoD to investigate or
identify the objects.

Given this limited involvement with the subject, the Ministry of
Defence has no department which is appointed solely for the
purpose of studying UFO reports, nor are there any staff employed
on the subject full time. The reports we receive, which are
usually limited to very brief details of what was witnessed, are
examined by staff in the departments concerned with the UK's air
defence as a part of their normal duties.

I enclose a report from our files which corresponds to the

time and location given in your letter. I regret that Iam
unable to offer a definitive explanation for the sightings
witnessed on 5 November. It is clear from the’ reports we receive
that there are many strange things to be seen in the sky. 2 90
However, we believe that rational explanations could be “found for
most of them. Possibilities that spring to mind include
meteorological balloons, satellite debris re-entering the
atmosphere, or aircraft seen from unusual angles, to name but a

few.

I hope this is helpful. _ .
